Ahiṁsā. (vide under Buddhists
Buddhism, Purāṇas, sacrifices), Devala quoted by Kalpataru says hiṁsā is of ten kinds, Dharmaśāstras and Purāṇas put great emphasis on A. ; emphasized in Upaniṣads like the Chāndogya, Dharmaśūtras, Mahābhārata, emphasized in the Purāṇas and sometimes unqualified A. ; examples of Vedic sacrifices (including animal sacrifices) performed durig some centuries before and after Christian era ; hiṁsā in fighting was allowed to Kṣatriyas by Manu and Yāj., but if one wanted to be a yogin he had to give it up, is door of Dharma or highest Dharma and that neither dāna nor tapas is equal to A. ; is one of the yamas, acc. to Kūrma, Yogāsūtra and Yāj. ; Kūrmapurāṇa and others stated that killing sanctioned by Veda is not hiṁsā ; some purāṇas sanction the killing of a person (such as a tyrant or desperado) by whose death many will live in peace, stated to be sanātanadharma by Matsya and Brahmāṇḍa ; Upaniṣads commended qualified A. pp. 944, 945, 946, 947, 948, 1028, 1029, 1420

Avatāras (vide ‘Bhāgavata’
‘Buddha’, ‘Reincarnation’), Agnipuraṇa describes the characteristics of the images of ten a. ; all ten avatāras of Viṣṇu had become recognized throughout India at least before 10th century A D. ; are dwelt upon at great length in Purāṇas ; Buddhists in the Mahāyāna teach-ings made Buddha go through many a. as Bodhisattva ; conception of, may be traced to the Śat. Br. ; conception of a. increased Dharmaśāstra material and gave rise to numerous vratas and festivals called Jayantīs , derivation and meaning of the word ; in modern times some persons pose or are made to pose as avatāras by their admirers ; Indian theory of a. is connected with the theory of yugas ; Kumārila (7th century A. D.) did not regard Buddha as avatāra of Viṣṇu ; Lakṣmī follows Viṣṇu in all avatāras ; Locus classicus on a. is Bhaga-vadgītā IV. 7-8, none of the ten avatāras except Kṛṣṇa and possibly Rāma is expressly named in Bhagavad-gītā ; of Viṣṇu due to a curse of Bhṛgu ; one Pak Subuh who hails from Indo-nesia is suggested by Mr. J. G. Bennett as an avatāra for whom mankind is waiting ; reference to Buddha as avatāra in Bhāgavata ; references from Purāṇas to each of ten a. ; ten a. of Viṣṇu in medie-val and modern times named in Matsya and Varāha Purāṇas ; thirtynine a. of Vāsudeva in Ahirbudhnya-samhitā ; thirty-three a. discussed by Dr. Katre ; tithis of the appe-arance of, Todarā-nanda probably contains long-est account of ten a. ; twelve a. noted in Matsya and Padma ; twenty-two a. of Viṣṇu in Bhāgavata ; Vāmana and Kṛṣṇa a. known centuries before Mahābhāṣya ; Varāha avatāra m. in Gupta Inscription in the first year of Toramāṇa (about to A. ## Kāḷā as the first principle in Atharvaveda
pp. 1495
## Kāḷā ( vide under Kalpa
Man-vantara, pralaya, Rudra, measures of Time, yuga); Āśvamedhikaparva has an elaborate metaphorical descri-ption of wheel of K. ; Bhagavad-gītā identifies K. with Kṛṣṇa ; derived from root ‘Kal’ in Bhagavad-gītā, included among nine *dravyās* by Vaiśeṣika-sūtra ; in the case of religious rites K. is not a mere detail, but is the *nimitta* ( occasion ) on the happening of which an act has to be performed and what is done at a time other than the prescribed one is as good as not done ; Mahābhārata frequently refers to theme of Kāḷā ; *mukhya* and *gauṇa* , not inclu-ded in the *tattvas* of Sāṅkhya, but m. in relation to Karaṇas ; Omkāra is said to be beyond the three divisions of K. by Māṇḍūkya Up. ; Pāṇini uses the word K. for 'time' in general or proper time or divisions of time ; Patañjali's impor-tant theory about K. that it does not exist except as a con-venient name or word for the manner of measuring changes and comparing them ; produced by Him who is inte-lligent and omniscient, accto Śv. Up., produces and destroys beings and is invincible, acc. to Ādiparva ; Purāṇas on K. hold that time is without beginning or end, is all-embracing and is identified with God ; Śāntiparva on the formidable influence of K. on all including even Indra and other gods ; Śāntiparva speaks of Kāla as 20th guṇa and to be the source and end of all beings ; Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa uses it in sense of time or 'proper' time ; Śvetāśvataropaniṣad uses the word Kāla in sense of cause or source of creation ; units of K. ff; Upaniṣads use the word K. in the sense of 'end' or 'proper time' or 'appointed time' ; Vanaparva identifies Kṛṣṇa with K., Brahma, Rudra etc, views on time being non-existent as a separate entity or as being directly apprehended or as merely inferred ; word occurs in Ṛg. only once ; word used in two senses even by early Vedic times viz. time in general and as identified with Supreme Being ; Yogasūtrabhāṣya has a brief but interesting and abstract disquisition on K. pp. 25, 73, 100, 102, 166, 463, 464, 465, 466, 467
##. Karka and Karkin-occur in Atharvaveda
pp. 563, 1121
## Karma and Punarjanma 1530-1612: (vide under Christianity
Empedocles, Heaven, Herodotus, Plato, Pythagoras, Prof. Ranade, 'reincarnation,' Vedanta, Upanisads) : antecedent supposition to belief in K. and P. are four ; Ap. Dh. S. and Gautama on ; belief had arisen in times of Sat. Br. that one who does evil deeds in one life has to suffer for them in a later life from that being which he harmed ; belief that punishment by the king for a bad deed relieved the doer of the sin ; Brahmana works (such as Sat. XII. 9. 1. 1) give details about the rewards of good works and the retribution for evil deeds ; Br. Up. IV. 4. 5-7 is the leading and the oldest Upanisad passage on K. and P. and so is Br. Up. III. 3. , confession of sins was in certain cases held to purify a person as in Varupa-praghāsa Cāturmāsya and necessary in prāyaścittas for some sins ; clear statement of the doctrine of K. and P. is absent from the Rgveda though it refers to two births of Vasistha ; difficulty of reconciling the doctrine of K. with the system of Sraddhas for ancestors ; doctrine of Karma leads on to doctrine of punarjanma ; doctrine of, extends the inexorable physical law of causality to the mental and moral sphere ; doctrine of, has attracted many minds from the West and there is now a voluminous literature on it in the West, doctrine of, modified doctrine of Heaven and Hell ; doctrine of Karma, instead of being a gospel of hope and an urge to effort, became in the minds of many Indians confused with fatalism ; doctrine of K. permeated the whole of society, poets and others ; doctrine of K. not pessimistic or fatalist if rightly understood but emphasizes all-out effort in this life ; doctrine of, received almost unanimous support from all darśanas (except Cārvāka) that severely criticize each other's doctrines ; doctrine of, touched upon by the Bhagavadgītā in many passages ; doctrine of, a unique characteristic of Hindu culture, effects of evil deeds done by a man can be got rid of only by expiations, or by suffering them in another life or by realization of Absolute brahman ; efforts made by a man on the path of Yoga are not altogether lost, but become steps on the path of perfection ; four kinds of, viz. krsna, sukla etc. ; idea of K. was prevalent among Jewish people in the time of Jesus (vide St. John IX. 2) ; idea of punar-mṛtyu in Śat. Br. and Tai. Br., Br. Up. and Kauṣ. Br. ; influenced Indian thought from at least Upaniṣad times and all Hindus, Buddhists and Jains ; is an endeavour to answer the question, what happens to men after death of body ; law of Karma, laid down by Upaniṣads, was inexorable that fruits of all actions, good or bad, must be experienced, but some exceptions were made, the first being that actions done after a person has realized brahman but before the body falls do not produce results ; little is said in the Ṛgveda about the fate of evil-doers ; McTaggart, L. P. Jacks, Wordsworth and some other Western writers favour the doctrine of K. ; Mahābhārata in some passages avers that the consequences of the evil deeds of a man are suffered by the man’s son or grandsons (and not by himself), Manu prescribes Japa of certain Vedic hymns for removal of even mortal sins and highly praises Aghamarṣaṇa hymn, men guilty of grave sins may, acc. to Chān. Up., Kathā Up., Manusmṛti become beasts, or even tree trunks but theosophists and some modern men say that once the state of a human body is reached there is no regre-ssion to a lower stage ; objections against doctrine of K. stated and replied ; objection that doctrine of K. leaves no scope for Free will, dealt with ; once prāyaścittas were admitted as removing effects of sins, other ways were prescribed in early times for the same purpose such as Japa (recitation of holy texts), homa, tapas, fast, gifts, pilgrimages ; passages in Manu, Yāj. and other Smṛtis that a guest when not properly honoured gives his demerit to the householder and takes away latter’s all puṇya or stating that if a witness deposes falsely all his merit in many lives will go to the party who lost that suit are mere arthavādas ; popular idea of last thought at death leading to a new birth appropriate to it is an interference with the law of K. ; Prāṇāyāma, practice of, for removal of effects of sins, Purāṇas emphasize the importance of good or evil deeds, that one has to reap the fruits of one’s actions, that Karma does not come to end even after many lives unless the results thereof are undergone ; Purāṇas went so far as to prescribe mere remembrance of the name of Kṛṣṇa or Nārāyaṇa, particularly for śūdras and pratiloma castes, for removal of effects of sins ; Ramayaṇa, on doctrine of K. ; Ṛgveda contains some prayers for wealth and heroic sons, but it was immortality and joys of heaven that were most valued ; Śatapatha Br. had arrived at the idea that man's will governs what he will reach after death ; Śatapatha Br. has a passage on the powers conferred in yonder world by sacrifices performed in this life such as Darśapūrṇamāsa, Cāturmāsya ; some features of our present life can be more satisfactorily explained on the theory of Karma than on any other ; strict doctrine of K. would require that one man's good or bad Karma cannot be transferred to another, but in Ṛg. fear is expressed that one may have to suffer for others’ deeds and modifications were introduced such as the king getting 1/6 in the merit or demerit of his subjects ; three classes of Karma, *sañcita*, *prārabdha* and *kriyamāṇa* (or '*sañcīyamāna*') explained ; three regular treatises in Sanskrit on K. mentioned and described ; Vedāntasūtra on K. and P. ; very few regular treatises in Sanskrit on the law of K. ; word Karma in some passages of Ṛg. means ‘exploits’, ‘valiant deeds’ and in some ‘religious works’ such as sacrifices and gifts, works and conduct done in this life fashion a man's future life and that the present existence of a man depends on his actions and conduct in a past life or lives — this is the gist of the grand passages of Bṛ. Up. IV. 4, 5-7 and III. 2, which are illumined by the illustrations of the caterpillar and the snake's slough, works in English on K. pp. 13, 1530, 1532, 1533, 1534, 1535, 1536, 1537, 1546, 1547

D. ; held the view that no word was significant in isolation but became significant when joined with a word or words in a sentence ; Jaiminīya-nyāya–mālā–vistāra cites points of difference between P. and Kumārila ; later than Kumārila, though no substantial evidence (except tradition) for holding that P. was a pupil of Kumārila ; quotes Kirātārjunīya pp. 15, 675, 725, 1189, 1190, 1192, 1195, 1198, 1296

Varṇa system and caste system : ( vide under brāhmaṇas
Cāṇdālas, caste, jātis, moral qualities, social legislation, śūdra) : origin and development of ; Bhagavadgītā ( IV. and XVIII. ) asserts that the Varṇa system was based on qualities and actions and specifies the qualities and actions of the four varṇas, most of which for brāhmaṇas and kṣatriyas are moral and spiritual ; how and why several thousands of castes and sub-castes arose only in India is an insoluble problem ; if Varṇa and caste system has broken down and become harmful and unpopular among people it may be discarded, but that cannot be done by force or mere legislation, but by strenuous work and education of all from childhood ; intermarriages between the first three varṇas were allowed even in the times of Yāj. and a few other Smṛtis , is distinguished from jāti, but the two are confounded in Manu, it may be conceded that some centuries before the Puruṣasūkta, Aryan community was divided into four groups, a similar division not being unnatural and being found in other countries ; no evidence to show that intermarriages and interdining among the four varṇas was prohibited in the Vedic age ; numerous artisans and craftsmen are mentioned in Vāj. S., Kāṭhaka S. and Tai. Br., but there is nothing to show that they had become petrified castes, Varṇas were only four and there was no fifth v. ; word varṇa was applied to āryas and dāsas in Ṛgveda, they being hostile camps, words brāhmaṇa and kṣatriya frequently occur in Ṛg, but the word varṇa is not expressly connected with them in it pp. 13, 42, 44, 1265, 1632, 1633, 1635, 1641, 1643

Veda: ( vide Mantras
Mahābhāṣya, nāmādheya, Ṛgveda, Pūrvamīmāṁsā ); all Veda texts do not contain vidhis, numerous V. passages are commendatory, or condemn an act that is prohibited or refer to instances of persons performing the vidhi in the past or put forward to be a reason for the vidhi; these are not to be treated as unnecessary, but are to be takeh along with vidhi passages to complete their full import ; arrangement into Maṇḍalas or Aṣṭakas or Kāṇḍas is not claimed to be eternal, though the V. is eternal , different interpretations of some mantras by Śabara, Kumārila, Nirukta, Mahābhāṣya, Sāyana exemplified in the case of Ṛg. IV.58.3, divided into five categories viz. vidhi (hortatory), mantras, nāmādheya (names like Udbhid), praśedha or niṣedha (prohibition) and arthavāda (laudatory or explanatory passages) and examples of these , divided into three parts, vidhi, arthavāda, mantra, (nāmadheya being placed under vidhi) ; the epic and Purāṇas say that the one eternal Veda was arranged into four parts by Vyāsa, but do not say that they were distributed into maṇḍalas or kāṇḍas by Vyāsa ; eternal, self-existent, not composed by any human or divine author and infallible, acc. to P. M. S. ; is constituted by Mantras and Brāhmaṇa ; Mantras of the three Vedas are said by Manu to have been drawn from Agni, Vāyu and Sūrya ; Manu lays down Japa (muttering) of Vedic mantras of adhiyajña, adhidaiva and adhyātma types ; many vedic texts are apparently in conflict with each other and, with ordinary experience ; many verses of Veda had threefold applications viz. with reference to sacrifices ( *adhiyajña* ), adhidaiva or adhidaivata ( with reference to deities ) and adhyatma ( with reference to metaphysical meaning ) and illustrations ; Naiyayikas hold that God is the author of the Veda and the pp. 839, 859, 861, 1098, 1124, 1181, 1202, 1204, 1222, 1225
Vedāntasūtra holds same view 1202; necessity of knowing four matters about every mantra viz the ṛṣi
the metre, the deity and application, emphasized by Ait Br., Chān

the first of which only conveys that the agent only exists (as in ‘asti’), the second of which conveys not only the existence of the agent but also some activity of the agent (as in yajati), that is, in these latter the sense of ‘karoti’ (he does) is also understood<sup>1237</sup>

Vidhi (or Vidhis) ; (vide under Bhāvanā
Niyama and Parisaṁkhyā): V. apply to all Āryas<sup>1281</sup>; (Vidhi) is an exhortation that is meaningful on account of enjoining a matter that has a useful purpose and that prescribes something which does not follow (is not established by) from any other authority ; is laid down by a verbal form or potential passive participle in ‘ya’ or ‘tavya’<sup>1225n</sup>; is ordinarily couched in the optative form (vidhilin) and a verb in the present tense cannot ordinarily be taken as laying down a V. ; only vidhis so called have absolute authority, while Arthavādas have authority in so far as they praise Vidhis ; one classification of vidhis is into four, sometimes a vidhi may be inferred even. from a verb in the present tense ( illustrated ) ; the central element in v. is the verb or verbal form expressed as ‘one shall do’, ‘it shall be done’, ‘it ought to be done’ ; the employment of words like ‘hi’ or ‘vai’ is not generally allowed in a V. ; the text about rātrisattra, though in the present tense is, construed as a v. ; three-fold classification of v. into apūrvavidhi ( as in svargakāmo yajeta ), niyamavidhi ( as in ‘he pounds rice’ ) and Pari-saṅkhyā ; two-fold classification of vidhis into Puruṣārtha and Kratvartha ; where a rite has already been laid down all that can be said as laid down about it later is to enjoin an accessary (guṇa, a detail ), where a vidhi is already, established a separate vidhi is required for each of the auxiliary matters relating to it pp. 1225, 1226, 1227, 1228, 1229, 1232, 1234, 1263, 1295

D. ; possibility that h. might lead to a more or less correct state-ment about a person's features but little about the vicissitudes of his life, principle that h was merely like a map or plan was often given up by Varāhamihira and others , Ptolemy insists that h. is not the sole basis for judg-ing a person's future, but country of birth, race, customs, upbringing have also to be considered ; some horo-scopes of avatāras like Rāma and of other famous ancient persons examined ; twelve houses in h., their names and synonyms ; Utpala and Rājamārtaṇḍa also require as Ptolemy does, the considera-tion of country, caste, family and customs of a person whose h. is to be explained , was linked with the doctrine of Karma and Punarjanma by Varāhamihira and other Indian astrologers ; word horo-scope coined late in Greece pp. 500, 545, 547, 548, 553, 554, 555, 567, 577, 578

dreṣkāṇa, horā, Mahābhārata, Nārāyaṇa, person, Rudra, Zodiac); are twelve, each extending over 2½ nakṣatras from Aśvinī onwards in order ; are identified from Meṣa onwards with the limbs of the Kālapuruṣa or of Nārāyaṇa ; as lords of the quarters (diśas) and usefulness thereof ; classifications of r, as male or female, movable and fixed, due to fancy and ideas of symmetry and sequence ; Chinese names of rāśis are different such as rat, ox, tiger etc. , colours of the twelve r. acc. to Bṛhaj-jātaka ; description of the appearance of r. in Bṛhaj-jātaka, in the Yavanajātaka q. by Utpala and in Vāmanapurāṇa ; descriptions of seven r. show that they are figures of animals and five are like human beings ; many scholars deny that there is similarity between the majority of the twelve signs in zodiac and the natural appearance of the objects signified by them ; mentioned by Vṛddha-Garga and Kāśyapa ; no Greek word corresponding to Mīna (Pisces) mentioned by Bṛhajjātaka in some editions ; names of signs among Babylonians, Assyrians and Chaldeans cited by several scholars do not tally ; names of r. are purely imaginary , no proof that the rāśi system was borrowed by India from the Greeks ; principles of astronomy based on r. probably established in India in the three centuries before Christ ; purpose of identification of r. with limbs of Kālapuruṣa ; purpose and astrological indications of the classifications of r. acc. to Utpala ; Sārāvalī mentions no Greek word for Mīna ; six items in relation to the r. occupied by a planet called Ṣaḍvarga ; table of r. as svagṛha, ucca and nīca of the planets ; table of r. with English and Latin equivalents and synonyms ; things supposed to be under the influence of the several r. ; what r. are powerful by day, at night and in twilight ; when are r. powerful pp. 561, 562, 563, 564, 565, 566, 568, 569, 576, 577

Rationalism : ( vide under doubt
God, people, Hinduism, persecution, science, tradition, utilitarianism ) : accepts the postulates which science finds convenient and useful but science works within narrow limits, it is not concerned with morals or spirituality or values of life <sup>1475</sup> ; in Europe emphasis on rationalism arose as the Christian churches, particularly Roman Catholic, were extremely intolerant of the slightest departure from religious views held at particular times <sup>1576-77</sup> ; there are subconscious and nonrational impulses, beliefs and intuitions in men that are held by men to be truer or of a higher order than what is on the rationalistic level <sup>1475</sup> ; thoughtful men in Europe during 18th and 19th centuries had boundless faith in r. and progress, but this faith is now greatly weakened <sup>1478</sup>
Rationalists ( vide 'Communism and Communists' ) ; r. arrive at different conclusions on the same matter in different ages and even in the same age what appears reasonable to one group is held unreasonable by another group <sup>1477</sup> ; argue that they should not have to prove negative propositions ( such as there is no God ) but theists must prove the positive proposition of God's existence etc. <sup>1473</sup> ; it should not be supposed that ancient and medieval India had no rationalists or atheists
in fact there were several r. such as the Lokāyatas and rationalistic critics of sacrifices, Śrāddhas etc. <sup>974 (note )</sup>, <sup>1472</sup> ; Bentham and the two Mills put forward the theory of utilitarianism <sup>1479</sup> ; many r. hold that there is no proof of the existence of God or any higher Intelligence than man's, deny individual soul and immortality, and argue that in all religions there is some truth with a great deal of error <sup>1472</sup> ; problem of evil in the world is, acc. to r., a great stumbling block in accepting God as good, kind, omnipotent and allknowing, <sup>1473</sup> ; r. and utilitarians are not agreed upon any definite principles of conduct for common men and women <sup>1480</sup> ; r. substitute blocks of humanity or the leaders of such blocks for authority and worship, the likely result being that humanity would soon be wiped out <sup>1474</sup> ; warning of Gītā against unsettling the minds of ignorant persons <sup>1480</sup> pp. 1596

Brāhmaṇa or Brāhmaṇas (vide under gifts
pātra, priests); all brāhmaṇas were not priests, are not priests in modern times and all temple priests are not always brāhmaṇas ; every b. was not a proper recipient for gifts in ancient Indian times ; difference of view on killing an ātatāyin b. ; gifts (religious) to be made acc. to śāstras, Manu and other Smṛtis only to pātra (deserving) br. who has learning, character and tapas, gifts made to brāhmaṇa not learned in Veda or who is avaricious or deceitful were deemed fruitless by Manu and to lead donor to hell ; had no power to raise taxes like tithes nor was there a regularly paid hierarchy of priests and bishops as in England, high ideal placed before br. of plain living, of giving up active pursuit of riches, of devotion to study, pp. , how importance of birth as a brāhmaṇa arose ; lores to be studied by a learned brāhmaṇa, acc. to Gautama Dh. S. ; maintaining themselves by practising astrology were unfit for being invited to perform rites for gods or for śrāddha dinner acc. to Manu ; Manu and others provided that in rites for pitṛs the learning and character of b. should be carefully scrutinized, but Purāṇas did away with this, particularly at Gayā ; of Mathurā are like gods, says Varāhapurāṇa ; Mahābhārata emphasizes that good character makes a brāhmaṇa ; milk is vrata of, most Br. studied only one Veda or a part of it, only br. were entitled to drink Soma beverage in Vedic sacrifices ; performed solemn Vedic sacrifices like Vājapeya ; reasons or basis of the veneration paid to brāhmaṇas for over three thousand years, result of threatening or striking or injuring (with blood from wound) a brāhmaṇa declared in Tai. S., which is interpreted in two ways by different writers, substantial sources of income were only three, viz. teaching, officiating as priests at sacrifices of others and receiving gifts made voluntarily which were fitful, variable and precarious, to learn vidyā from a kṣatriya was unnatural or unusual in Upaniṣad times, to be fed in all vratas, tribute paid in passing to the ancient brāhmaṇa ideal by Pandit Jawaharlal Nehru ; vast Vedic and classical literature was cultivated, preserved and propagated mostly by brāhmaṇas with arduous labour and the Ṛgveda was preserved by them with unparalleled care, were asked to study Veda and subsidiary lores without any eye to motives of profit as a duty and to understand it, to teach the Veda without demanding a fee beforehand, to perform sacrifices and to make gifts, who is not a Vaiṣṇava is declared as a heretic by Padmapurāṇa and Vṛddha-Hārīta ; Yāj. provides that even a learned br. should refuse gifts and that the king should donate to learned brāhmaṇas, land, houses and marriage expenses or br. should approach a king or rich person for their 'Yogakṣema' pp. 18, 39, 100, 527, 817, 818, 853, 864, 930, 932
Brāhmaṇa or Brāhmaṇas (texts
acc. to context), all those parts of the Veda which are not or cannot be called mantras constitute B.<sup>1222–23</sup>; are primary sources of information about sacrifices, ritual, priests etc. ; contain oldest prose found in any Indo-European language ; contain numerous myths and legends, contents can be divided into two classes viz. vidhis (hortatory) and arthavādas (explanatory or laudatory) ; often mention contests between Gods and Asuras and abound in etymologies ; order (krama) of rites as expressed in mantras is to be preferred to the order contained in Br. texts ; principal purpose and aim of Br. of the Veda is to prescribe the rituals of sacrifices, to narrate myths and legends about sacrifices and to state rewards of sacrifices, ten characteristics of B. acc. to Vṛttikāra pp. 1223, 1316, 1497

Yantra (geometrical diagram) or yantras (vide under cakra) 1135-1139; a characteristic in Tantra worship
sometimes called cakra ; bindus in the diagram of Śrīcakra represents Śakti or mūlaprakṛti solidified ; derivation of the word ‘Yantra’ ; described in many tantra works and. some Purāṇas ; difference between Maṇḍala and Y. ; differently enumerated in different works ; distinction between Y. and devatā is like that between body and soul, is engraved, drawn or painted on metal, stone, cloth, paper, birch leaf or other material with paste of sandalwood, camphor, musk, saffron and should be worn on head , Kulārṇavatantra and others say that the devatā is not pleased if it be worshipped without Y. ; most noted Y.. is Śrīcakra ; Prapañcasāra tantra states that by the worship of a certain Y. a sādhaka can make a woman smitten with passion for him and draw her to him , prescribed by even Śāradātilaka for destruction of one’s enemies ; Śrīcakra, described ; special ‘Yantragāyatri’ evolved by some works ; used for making holy the bed of a woman in labour ; was supposed to restrain restlessness of mind due to anger, love etc. and to induce concentration ; worship of Yantra is bahiraṅga-pūjā ( external worship of Śakti ), while antaryāga consists in taking awakened Kuṇḍalini from Mūlādhāra through all Cakras ; Zimmer on Y. in ‘Myths and symbols in Indian Art’ pp. 1047, 1105, 1135, 1136, 1137, 1138, 1139

Year: (vide Bārhaspatya cycle; Samvatsara): beginning of Indian Y. now in either Caitra or Kārtika but in different months in ancient times 659-60; Bārhaspatya year 658; Bārhaspatya year names adopted at the beginning of a year in N. brahmacarya): belief in Śānti-parva and of Śaṅkarācārya that Y. could transfer himself to other bodies ; belief that Y. can vanish from people, see distant objects and hear in spite of great distance ; duties of Sannyāsin in Manu are applicable to Y. ; greatest emphasis laid on chas-tity in thought, word and deed for the Y. or the seeker after brahman ; has to master sleep (nidrā) also, and n; has to stick to aparigraha ; honour and disrespect should be treated by Y. as poison or nectar respectively ; if individual Yogin possesses a tolerably sound body he need not devote time to āsanās but may proceed with prāṇāyāma ; is said to repeat the Ajapā-japa by breathing in and out ; places to be resorted to for practice of Yoga by Y. and to be avoided ; proper behaviour for Y. (yogī-caryā) acc. to Mārkaṇḍeya and Viṣṇu-purāṇas ; practitioner of Yoga is asked by Gītā (VI. ) to gaze steadily at the tip of his nose ; purity of mind follows on purity of food acc. to Chān. Up. acc. to context): ; (vide under Cakrapūjā, China, guhya, guru, Kauladharma, magic, magicians, Mahānirvāṇatantra, makāras, months, nāḍis, puraścaraṇa, śakti, śāktas, Śāradātilaka, siddhis, Śrīvidyāmantra, spells, tāntrika, tattva, Tibet, Vajrayāna vāmācāra, yantra) ; aims of T. were mokṣa and attainment of siddhis, are Hindu, Buddhist and Jaina and are alike in some respects but differ in some others , are upaveda of Atharvaveda, acc. to Śukranītisāra ; Bhairavi-cakra, and tattva cakra worship and n; Buddhist T. deified important personages of Buddhism and took over other deities, Gaṇeśa, Sarasvati and others ; Buddhist T. also claimed to show the path to the attainment of all objects from success in love affairs to liberation ; Cakrapūjā, a revolting rite ; derivation of word Tantra ; differences and similarities between Buddhist and Hindu tantras ; difficulty of defining a T. ; Durgā acc. to Mahānirvāṇa T., is highest prakṛti of Paramātman, has various names ; end and aim of a few higher minds among some tantras and Tāntrikas was attainment of high spiritual powers by Yoga practices and realization of supreme Tattva, variously called Brahma, Śiva, Devī and mokṣa ; evidence of any Buddhist works on Tantras before A. D. practically none, evidence in Bāṇa's works of the prevalence of Tantrik and Śākta worship in India before 7th century A. Avalon that they came to India from Chaldea or Śakadvīpa, examined , Hindu T. try to show that they are based on the Vedas. quote Vedic mantras and claim to describe an easier and quicker path to mokṣa ; Hindu T. present two sides, one philosophical, the other popular and magical, relying on mantras, mudrās, cakras and yantras, ignore the great moral danger, involved in the persistent teaching of worship with five makāras and also ignore the advice of Gītā ( III. ), in Kaula system siddhi results from those very substances ( wine, flesh etc. ) by ( resorting to ) which men ( ordinarily ) incur sin , influenced by Sāṅkhya ; important Kashmir Tāutrik works ; Kṣetras of Devi over one hundred acc. to Devībhāgavata, Kulārṇava and other T. praise the vedas; employ vedic mantras and quote Upaniṣads and Bhagavadgītā ; list of Sanskrit works on T. with authors, dates and editions, list of works and papers on T. in English ; literature on T. was vast ; Makāras five, emphasis on, in almost all Tantra works as leading to miraculous powers and liberation, Makāras explained by later writers on T. as used not in the ordinary senses but in special esoteric senses on which the writers themselves differ ; Mantras called bīja like hrīm´ and śrīm and a list of them called Bījanighapṭu ; Mantra of ten syllables ´ hrīm śrīm krīm parameśvarī svāhā. by merely listening to which a man becomes jīvanmukta but Mahānirvāṇa adds that mantras do not confer siddhi unless five makāras are offered ; Maṇḍalas, an item in T. worship, 1131ff; Mantras are the very core of T. and T. is often called Mantraśāstra ; Mantras of Śakti do not confer perfection · unless one follows kula practices ; mantras that the followers of Vāmamārga may employ about three makāras ( wine, mudrā and coitus ) ; Mantra ‘saccidekam brahma’ is best ; mantra ‘brahmārpapam brahma’ ( Bhagavadgītā IV. ) to be recited when offering wine and other tattvas to the highest pp. 10, 18, 21, 24, 64, 650, 800, 1031, 1033, 1034

Tarka (ratiocination) and Dharmaśāstra 1468-1482; (vide ‘Nyāya’
‘people’, ‘tolerance’, ‘Upaniṣads’) ; as an aṅga of Yoga , Buddhists and Jains were declared to be heterodox not because they resorted to T. but because they did not at all acknowledge the authority of the Veda and the sacred tradition ; Manu provides that none but he who considers the dicta of (vedic) sages, the rules of dharma (in smṛtis) and takes the help of tarka not in direct conflict with Veda and śāstras knows what Dharma is and this is the position of most orthodox Sanskrit writers about Tarka, Śaṅkarācārya’s position about T. ; Smṛtis lay down that blindly following the words of śāstra would lead to loss of Dharma and that in case of conflict between two Smṛtis reasoning should be resorted to, smṛtis and ancient Sanskrit writers have been very tolerant of differing views even on fundamental matters and brought about great changes in rituals, philosophical views, social customs, without persecution ; T. alone cannot give final answers or answers acceptable to most people on such questions as the existence of God or of the individual soul, or on what happens after death ; T. favoured by or not opposed to Śruti is acceptable to Br. Up. acc. to Śaṅkarācārya ; within the limits laid down by Śaṅkarācārya and other orthodox Sanskrit writers there was room enough for an enormous and bewildering variety of convictions, rites and practices to claim orthodoxy and examples of these ; wise man who is pure should, in deciding upon dharma and adharma, act after relying on his own' intelligence (reason) ; word 'tarka' occurs in Kaṭho-paniṣad ; Yaj.
